========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, I... I guess I'm here because everything feels like it's completely unraveling, to be honest. It all really started when my in-laws moved into our house. We thought it would be manageable, you know, family first and we have the space, but ever since they brought their boxes in, my entire sense of peace is just gone. 

I’ve been so incredibly keyed up all the time, just this constant, buzzing tension from the minute I wake up, and at the exact same time, I'm just completely worn down. I have absolutely zero patience left. I’m snapping at my wife constantly over the smallest things, which isn't fair to her and just creates more tension in the house, but I just have no fuse left at all. 

But what really scared me, what actually made me pick up the phone to make this appointment, is how it's destroying my work. I'm a talent manager, mostly for musicians, and my whole career is built on being the guy who handles things, the guy who is always on the ball. But lately? I’m looking at my phone ringing with calls I would normally handle immediately, and I’m just... avoiding them. I just sit there and watch it go to voicemail. It got so bad last week that I nearly let a major contract for one of my top musicians completely fall apart. I was supposed to finalize the terms, and I just sat there staring at the paperwork because I literally couldn’t think straight. My brain was just static. If I start losing my clients because I can't even function, I really don't know what I'm going to do.

No, no, I wouldn't say I've had anything like that. I mean, I'm definitely tense, like I said, just this constant buzzing feeling, and my heart might beat a little faster when I hear my mother-in-law's footsteps coming down the hall—which, by the way, she has this very distinct, heavy walk that I can hear from anywhere in the house, it's maddening—but it's not like a sudden attack of terror or anything. I don't feel like I'm having a heart attack or like I'm suddenly dying, if that's what you mean. It's just that my baseline anxiety is so high right now that I can't think straight. But no sudden spikes out of nowhere, no. Just the constant, overwhelming pressure of them being in my space, rearranging my kitchen, making passive-aggressive comments about how I load the dishwasher... it's just always there, humming in the background, making it impossible to focus on my clients.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean... not exactly in the way you might think. It's not like I'm terrified to go to the grocery store or drive my car or anything like that. But, if I'm being completely honest, and I guess that's why I'm paying to be here, I am definitely avoiding my own house as much as humanly possible. Which is ridiculous, right? It's my house. I pay the mortgage. But the second I pull into the driveway and see their car parked there—usually taking up way too much space, by the way, because my father-in-law cannot park straight to save his life—I just feel this knot in my stomach tighten up. 

So, I've started finding excuses to stay at the office later and later. I'll tell my wife I have to review contracts, or that I'm waiting on a call from a promoter on the West Coast, which, sure, happens sometimes, but lately, I'm just sitting at my desk, staring at the wall, just to delay having to walk through my own front door. And even when I am home, I'm avoiding the common areas. I'll hide out in our bedroom, pretending I have to take a work call, just so I don't have to sit in the living room and listen to my mother-in-law critique whatever we're watching on TV. 

And work... I mentioned work before, right? I'm definitely avoiding things there, too. Not the physical office, but the actual work. Like I said, I'm avoiding phone calls. I'm avoiding emails from clients who I know are going to be demanding, because I just don't have the mental bandwidth to deal with them right now. Usually, I love the back-and-forth, the negotiation, the putting out fires—that's what makes me good at my job. But right now? If a client emails me with a problem, I just close the laptop. I can't even look at it. I just want to put my head down and make it all stop. So, yeah, I guess I'm avoiding a lot, just... not necessarily places, if that makes sense. Just the situations that require me to actually engage and deal with people.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I... I guess I'm here because everything feels like it's completely unraveling, to be honest. I have absolutely zero patience left. I’m snapping at my wife constantly over the smallest things, which isn't fair to her and just creates more tension in the house, but I just have no fuse left at all. \n\nBut what really scared me, what actually made me pick up the phone to make this appointment, is how it's destroying my work. I'm a talent manager, mostly for musicians, and my whole career is built on being the guy who handles things, the guy who is always on the ball. But lately? I’m looking at my phone ringing with calls I would normally handle immediately, and I’m just... avoiding them. I just sit there and watch it go to voicemail. It got so bad last week that I nearly let a major contract for one of my top musicians completely fall apart. I was supposed to finalize the terms, and I just sat there staring at the paperwork because I literally couldn’t think straight. My brain was just static. If I start losing my clients because I can't even function, I really don't know what I'm going to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I've had anything like that. I mean, I'm definitely tense, like I said, just this constant buzzing feeling, and my heart might beat a little faster when I hear my mother-in-law's footsteps coming down the hall—which, by the way, she has this very distinct, heavy walk that I can hear from anywhere in the house, it's maddening—but it's not like a sudden attack of terror or anything. It doesn't peak like that. It's more just... a continuous, exhausting layer of stress that never, ever lets up. \n\nI don't feel like I'm having a heart attack or like I'm suddenly dying, if that's what you mean. It's just that my baseline anxiety is so high right now that I can't think straight. But no sudden spikes out of nowhere, no. Just the constant, overwhelming pressure of them being in my space, rearranging my kitchen, making passive-aggressive comments about how I load the dishwasher... it's just always there, humming in the background, making it impossible to focus on my clients.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, activities, or situations because of your feelings of tension or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... not exactly in the way you might think. It's not like I'm terrified to go to the grocery store or drive my car or anything like that. But, if I'm being completely honest, and I guess that's why I'm paying to be here, I am definitely avoiding my own house as much as humanly possible. Which is ridiculous, right? It's my house. I pay the mortgage. But the second I pull into the driveway and see their car parked there—usually taking up way too much space, by the way, because my father-in-law cannot park straight to save his life—I just feel this knot in my stomach tighten up. \n\nSo, I've started finding excuses to stay at the office later and later. I'll tell my wife I have to review contracts, or that I'm waiting on a call from a promoter on the West Coast, which, sure, happens sometimes, but lately, I'm just sitting at my desk, staring at the wall, just to delay having to walk through my own front door. And even when I am home, I'm avoiding the common areas. I'll hide out in our bedroom, pretending I have to take a work call, just so I don't have to sit in the living room and listen to my mother-in-law critique whatever we're watching on TV. \n\nAnd work... I mentioned work before, right? I'm definitely avoiding things there, too. Not the physical office, but the actual work. Like I said, I'm avoiding phone calls. I'm avoiding emails from clients who I know are going to be demanding, because I just don't have the mental bandwidth to deal with them right now. Usually, I love the back-and-forth, the negotiation, the putting out fires—that's what makes me good at my job. But right now? If a client emails me with a problem, I just close the laptop. I can't even look at it. I just want to put my head down and make it all stop. So, yeah, I guess I'm avoiding a lot, just... not necessarily places, if that makes sense. Just the situations that require me to actually engage and deal with people.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been experiencing any feelings of sadness or hopelessness lately?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sadness? Hopelessness? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, it's not like I'm sitting around crying or feeling like life isn't worth living or anything dramatic like that. It's really not sadness at all. If anything, it's just... frustration. Pure, unadulterated frustration. 

I don't feel hopeless, exactly, because I know logically that they can't live with us forever, right? I mean, surely there's an end date to this, even if my wife won't commit to one when I try to bring it up. She just says, "They're family, we have to help them right now," which, fine, I get it, but what about helping me? What about my sanity? So it's not hopeless, it's just... I'm just so angry and on edge all the time. 

I'm not depressed, I don't think. I still want to do things, I still want to be successful at my job—I mean, I'm terrified of losing my clients, which wouldn't happen if I didn't care, right? If I were hopeless, I'd just let the contracts expire and not give a damn. But I do care. I care a lot. It's just that I'm so bogged down by this constant buzzing anxiety and irritation that I can't execute. So, no, definitely not sad. Just incredibly, overwhelmingly stressed out and resentful that my sanctuary has been completely hijacked by people who think it's perfectly acceptable to rearrange my spice rack alphabetically when I specifically had it organized by cuisine. Who does that? It's infuriating, not sad.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Outside of my family? Yeah. I mean, absolutely. It's bleeding into literally everything. You'd think I could compartmentalize, right? That's what I do for a living, I manage artists, I handle crises, I keep the drama out of the business. But right now... it's like the dam broke. 

Just last week—and this is what really terrified me, honestly—I nearly lost a massive contract for a musician I represent. He's this incredibly talented guy, but very high maintenance, always tweaking his tour rider, and usually, I am all over those details. I catch everything. But I was so distracted, just sitting there at my desk fuming about my father-in-law leaving his golf clubs in the middle of the hallway again, that I completely missed a major exclusivity clause in the promoter's draft. We almost lost the entire deal. 

And because of that, my managing partner... we've known each other for fifteen years, we came up in the agency together, we've always had each other's backs... he actually pulled me into his office. He sat me down and looked me in the eye and questioned whether I could still be trusted with major negotiations right now. Me! I'm the closer. I'm the guy they bring in to seal the deal. To hear him say that, to see the doubt in his face... it was humiliating. But the worst part is, I couldn't even defend myself because I knew he was right. I'm totally dropping the ball. 

And it all comes back to the house. The conflict there has just gotten so intense, so completely unmanageable. My wife and I, we used to be such a solid team, but now every single interaction we have turns into a fight about her parents. It's gotten serious enough that she's actually the one who pushed for outside help. She basically told me I needed to go talk to someone—which is why I'm sitting here with you—because she can't handle how on edge I am, and I can't handle her parents taking over our lives, and... it's just a mess. My career, my marriage, it feels like it's all imploding just because I can't get a single moment of peace in my own home. So, yeah, it's definitely affecting my relationships across the board.

I was supposed to finalize the terms, and I just sat there staring at the paperwork because I literally couldn’t think straight. My brain was just static. If I start losing my clients because I can't even function, I really don't know what I'm going to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I've had anything like that. I mean, I'm definitely tense, like I said, just this constant buzzing feeling, and my heart might beat a little faster when I hear my mother-in-law's footsteps coming down the hall—which, by the way, she has this very distinct, heavy walk that I can hear from anywhere in the house, it's maddening—but it's not like a sudden attack of terror or anything. It doesn't peak like that. It's more just... a continuous, exhausting layer of stress that never, ever lets up. \n\nI don't feel like I'm having a heart attack or like I'm suddenly dying, if that's what you mean. It's just that my baseline anxiety is so high right now that I can't think straight. But no sudden spikes out of nowhere, no. Just the constant, overwhelming pressure of them being in my space, rearranging my kitchen, making passive-aggressive comments about how I load the dishwasher... it's just always there, humming in the background, making it impossible to focus on my clients.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, activities, or situations because of your feelings of tension or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... not exactly in the way you might think. It's not like I'm terrified to go to the grocery store or drive my car or anything like that. But, if I'm being completely honest, and I guess that's why I'm paying to be here, I am definitely avoiding my own house as much as humanly possible. Which is ridiculous, right? It's my house. I pay the mortgage. But the second I pull into the driveway and see their car parked there—usually taking up way too much space, by the way, because my father-in-law cannot park straight to save his life—I just feel this knot in my stomach tighten up. \n\nSo, I've started finding excuses to stay at the office later and later. I'll tell my wife I have to review contracts, or that I'm waiting on a call from a promoter on the West Coast, which, sure, happens sometimes, but lately, I'm just sitting at my desk, staring at the wall, just to delay having to walk through my own front door. And even when I am home, I'm avoiding the common areas. I'll hide out in our bedroom, pretending I have to take a work call, just so I don't have to sit in the living room and listen to my mother-in-law critique whatever we're watching on TV. \n\nAnd work... I mentioned work before, right? I'm definitely avoiding things there, too. Not the physical office, but the actual work. Like I said, I'm avoiding phone calls. I'm avoiding emails from clients who I know are going to be demanding, because I just don't have the mental bandwidth to deal with them right now. Usually, I love the back-and-forth, the negotiation, the putting out fires—that's what makes me good at my job. But right now? If a client emails me with a problem, I just close the laptop. I can't even look at it. I just want to put my head down and make it all stop. So, yeah, I guess I'm avoiding a lot, just... not necessarily places, if that makes sense. Just the situations that require me to actually engage and deal with people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of sadness or hopelessness lately?\n\nPatient: Sadness? Hopelessness? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, it's not like I'm sitting around crying or feeling like life isn't worth living or anything dramatic like that. It's really not sadness at all. If anything, it's just... frustration. Pure, unadulterated frustration. \n\nI don't feel hopeless, exactly, because I know logically that they can't live with us forever, right? I mean, surely there's an end date to this, even if my wife won't commit to one when I try to bring it up. She just says, \"They're family, we have to help them right now,\" which, fine, I get it, but what about helping me? What about my sanity? So it's not hopeless, it's just... I'm just so angry and on edge all the time. \n\nI'm not depressed, I don't think. I still want to do things, I still want to be successful at my job—I mean, I'm terrified of losing my clients, which wouldn't happen if I didn't care, right? If I were hopeless, I'd just let the contracts expire and not give a damn. But I do care. I care a lot. It's just that I'm so bogged down by this constant buzzing anxiety and irritation that I can't execute. So, no, definitely not sad. Just incredibly, overwhelmingly stressed out and resentful that my sanctuary has been completely hijacked by people who think it's perfectly acceptable to rearrange my spice rack alphabetically when I specifically had it organized by cuisine. Who does that? It's infuriating, not sad.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty maintaining your relationships with others outside of your family due to your current stress and anxiety?\n\nPatient: Outside of my family? Yeah. I mean, absolutely. It's bleeding into literally everything. You'd think I could compartmentalize, right? That's what I do for a living, I manage artists, I handle crises, I keep the drama out of the business. But right now... it's like the dam broke. \n\nJust last week—and this is what really terrified me, honestly—I nearly lost a massive contract for a musician I represent. He's this incredibly talented guy, but very high maintenance, always tweaking his tour rider, and usually, I am all over those details. I catch everything. But I was so distracted, just sitting there at my desk fuming about my father-in-law leaving his golf clubs in the middle of the hallway again, that I completely missed a major exclusivity clause in the promoter's draft. We almost lost the entire deal. \n\nAnd because of that, my managing partner... we've known each other for fifteen years, we came up in the agency together, we've always had each other's backs... he actually pulled me into his office. He sat me down and looked me in the eye and questioned whether I could still be trusted with major negotiations right now. Me! I'm the closer. I'm the guy they bring in to seal the deal. To hear him say that, to see the doubt in his face... it was humiliating. But the worst part is, I couldn't even defend myself because I knew he was right. I'm totally dropping the ball. \n\nAnd it all comes back to the house. The conflict there has just gotten so intense, so completely unmanageable. My wife and I, we used to be such a solid team, but now every single interaction we have turns into a fight about her parents. It's gotten serious enough that she's actually the one who pushed for outside help. She basically told me I needed to go talk to someone—which is why I'm sitting here with you—because she can't handle how on edge I am, and I can't handle her parents taking over our lives, and... it's just a mess. Oh my god, yes. A thousand percent yes. If I could just get my house back, I know I would go back to normal almost instantly. I'd have my sanctuary back. But the thing you have to understand, the whole reason I'm in such a state right now, is that they are still there. 

This isn't some past event that I'm just holding onto or stewing over. The living arrangement hasn't ended. It is an active, ongoing situation. I mean, if they had moved out months ago and I was still missing exclusivity clauses in major contracts and hiding in my bedroom, then yeah, maybe I'd think I had some deep-seated psychological issue we'd need to unpack. I'd be looking at myself going, what is wrong with you, man, the crisis is over! But the crisis isn't over. The threat is still inside the house, literally. 

Every single morning I wake up and hear the coffee grinder going at 5:30 AM because my mother-in-law is an early riser, and my chest just tightens up all over again before I've even opened my eyes. Every evening, I'm dreading pulling into the driveway because I know they're inside. So, yes, my anxiety and the tension in my shoulders, all of it would absolutely plummet if they just packed up and left. 

But since my wife refuses to set a hard move-out date, they are still living with us indefinitely. I have no timeline. In my business, you always have a timeline—a tour ends, a contract expires, a promotional cycle wraps up. There's always a light at the end of the tunnel. Here? Nothing. So all this stress, all these problems at work and the fights with my wife, they're happening right now because I am currently, actively living in this scenario every single day. It hasn't been resolved. If it were resolved, I wouldn't even be sitting in this chair right now, I can promise you that.

I’m snapping at my wife constantly over the smallest things, which isn't fair to her and just creates more tension in the house, but I just have no fuse left at all. \n\nBut what really scared me, what actually made me pick up the phone to make this appointment, is how it's destroying my work. I'm a talent manager, mostly for musicians, and my whole career is built on being the guy who handles things, the guy who is always on the ball. But lately? I’m looking at my phone ringing with calls I would normally handle immediately, and I’m just... avoiding them. I just sit there and watch it go to voicemail. I was supposed to finalize the terms, and I just sat there staring at the paperwork because I literally couldn’t think straight. My brain was just static. If I start losing my clients because I can't even function, I really don't know what I'm going to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I've had anything like that. I mean, I'm definitely tense, like I said, just this constant buzzing feeling, and my heart might beat a little faster when I hear my mother-in-law's footsteps coming down the hall—which, by the way, she has this very distinct, heavy walk that I can hear from anywhere in the house, it's maddening—but it's not like a sudden attack of terror or anything. It doesn't peak like that. It's more just... a continuous, exhausting layer of stress that never, ever lets up. \n\nI don't feel like I'm having a heart attack or like I'm suddenly dying, if that's what you mean. It's just that my baseline anxiety is so high right now that I can't think straight. But no sudden spikes out of nowhere, no. Just the constant, overwhelming pressure of them being in my space, rearranging my kitchen, making passive-aggressive comments about how I load the dishwasher... it's just always there, humming in the background, making it impossible to focus on my clients.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, activities, or situations because of your feelings of tension or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... not exactly in the way you might think. It's not like I'm terrified to go to the grocery store or drive my car or anything like that. But, if I'm being completely honest, and I guess that's why I'm paying to be here, I am definitely avoiding my own house as much as humanly possible. Which is ridiculous, right? It's my house. I pay the mortgage. But the second I pull into the driveway and see their car parked there—usually taking up way too much space, by the way, because my father-in-law cannot park straight to save his life—I just feel this knot in my stomach tighten up. \n\nSo, I've started finding excuses to stay at the office later and later. I'll tell my wife I have to review contracts, or that I'm waiting on a call from a promoter on the West Coast, which, sure, happens sometimes, but lately, I'm just sitting at my desk, staring at the wall, just to delay having to walk through my own front door. And even when I am home, I'm avoiding the common areas. I'll hide out in our bedroom, pretending I have to take a work call, just so I don't have to sit in the living room and listen to my mother-in-law critique whatever we're watching on TV. \n\nAnd work... I mentioned work before, right? I'm definitely avoiding things there, too. Not the physical office, but the actual work. Like I said, I'm avoiding phone calls. I'm avoiding emails from clients who I know are going to be demanding, because I just don't have the mental bandwidth to deal with them right now. Usually, I love the back-and-forth, the negotiation, the putting out fires—that's what makes me good at my job. But right now? If a client emails me with a problem, I just close the laptop. I can't even look at it. I just want to put my head down and make it all stop. So, yeah, I guess I'm avoiding a lot, just... not necessarily places, if that makes sense. Just the situations that require me to actually engage and deal with people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of sadness or hopelessness lately?\n\nPatient: Sadness? Hopelessness? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, it's not like I'm sitting around crying or feeling like life isn't worth living or anything dramatic like that. It's really not sadness at all. If anything, it's just... frustration. Pure, unadulterated frustration. \n\nI don't feel hopeless, exactly, because I know logically that they can't live with us forever, right? I mean, surely there's an end date to this, even if my wife won't commit to one when I try to bring it up. She just says, \"They're family, we have to help them right now,\" which, fine, I get it, but what about helping me? What about my sanity? So it's not hopeless, it's just... I'm just so angry and on edge all the time. \n\nI'm not depressed, I don't think. I still want to do things, I still want to be successful at my job—I mean, I'm terrified of losing my clients, which wouldn't happen if I didn't care, right? If I were hopeless, I'd just let the contracts expire and not give a damn. But I do care. I care a lot. It's just that I'm so bogged down by this constant buzzing anxiety and irritation that I can't execute. So, no, definitely not sad. Just incredibly, overwhelmingly stressed out and resentful that my sanctuary has been completely hijacked by people who think it's perfectly acceptable to rearrange my spice rack alphabetically when I specifically had it organized by cuisine. Who does that? It's infuriating, not sad.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty maintaining your relationships with others outside of your family due to your current stress and anxiety?\n\nPatient: Outside of my family? Yeah. I mean, absolutely. It's bleeding into literally everything. You'd think I could compartmentalize, right? That's what I do for a living, I manage artists, I handle crises, I keep the drama out of the business. But right now... it's like the dam broke. \n\nJust last week—and this is what really terrified me, honestly—I nearly lost a massive contract for a musician I represent. He's this incredibly talented guy, but very high maintenance, always tweaking his tour rider, and usually, I am all over those details. I catch everything. But I was so distracted, just sitting there at my desk fuming about my father-in-law leaving his golf clubs in the middle of the hallway again, that I completely missed a major exclusivity clause in the promoter's draft. We almost lost the entire deal. \n\nAnd because of that, my managing partner... we've known each other for fifteen years, we came up in the agency together, we've always had each other's backs... he actually pulled me into his office. He sat me down and looked me in the eye and questioned whether I could still be trusted with major negotiations right now. Me! I'm the closer. I'm the guy they bring in to seal the deal. To hear him say that, to see the doubt in his face... it was humiliating. But the worst part is, I couldn't even defend myself because I knew he was right. I'm totally dropping the ball. \n\nAnd it all comes back to the house. The conflict there has just gotten so intense, so completely unmanageable. My wife and I, we used to be such a solid team, but now every single interaction we have turns into a fight about her parents. It's gotten serious enough that she's actually the one who pushed for outside help. She basically told me I needed to go talk to someone—which is why I'm sitting here with you—because she can't handle how on edge I am, and I can't handle her parents taking over our lives, and... it's just a mess. My career, my marriage, it feels like it's all imploding just because I can't get a single moment of peace in my own home. So, yeah, it's definitely affecting my relationships across the board.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety and tension would significantly decrease if your in-laws were no longer living with you?\n\nPatient: Oh my god, yes. A thousand percent yes. If I could just get my house back, I know I would go back to normal almost instantly. I'd have my sanctuary back. But the thing you have to understand, the whole reason I'm in such a state right now, is that they are still there. \n\nThis isn't some past event that I'm just holding onto or stewing over. The living arrangement hasn't ended. It is an active, ongoing situation. I mean, if they had moved out months ago and I was still missing exclusivity clauses in major contracts and hiding in my bedroom, then yeah, maybe I'd think I had some deep-seated psychological issue we'd need to unpack. I'd be looking at myself going, what is wrong with you, man, the crisis is over! But the crisis isn't over. The threat is still inside the house, literally. \n\nEvery single morning I wake up and hear the coffee grinder going at 5:30 AM because my mother-in-law is an early riser, and my chest just tightens up all over again before I've even opened my eyes. Every evening, I'm dreading pulling into the driveway because I know they're inside. So, yes, my anxiety and the tension in my shoulders, all of it would absolutely plummet if they just packed up and left. \n\nBut since my wife refuses to set a hard move-out date, they are still living with us indefinitely. I have no timeline. In my business, you always have a timeline—a tour ends, a contract expires, a promotional cycle wraps up. There's always a light at the end of the tunnel. Here? Nothing. So all this stress, all these problems at work and the fights with my wife, they're happening right now because I am currently, actively living in this scenario every single day. It hasn't been resolved. If it were resolved, I wouldn't even be sitting in this chair right now, I can promise you that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I'm just so angry and on edge all the time. \n\nI'm not depressed, I don't think. I still want to do things, I still want to be successful at my job—I mean, I'm terrified of losing my clients, which wouldn't happen if I didn't care, right? If I were hopeless, I'd just let the contracts expire and not give a damn. But I do care. I care a lot. It's just that I'm so bogged down by this constant buzzing anxiety and irritation that I can't execute. So, no, definitely not sad. Just incredibly, overwhelmingly stressed out and resentful that my sanctuary has been completely hijacked by people who think it's perfectly acceptable to rearrange my spice rack alphabetically when I specifically had it organized by cuisine. Who does that? It's infuriating, not sad.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty maintaining your relationships with others outside of your family due to your current stress and anxiety?\n\nPatient: Outside of my family? Yeah. I mean, absolutely. It's bleeding into literally everything. You'd think I could compartmentalize, right? That's what I do for a living, I manage artists, I handle crises, I keep the drama out of the business. But right now... it's like the dam broke. \n\nJust last week—and this is what really terrified me, honestly—I nearly lost a massive contract for a musician I represent. He's this incredibly talented guy, but very high maintenance, always tweaking his tour rider, and usually, I am all over those details. I catch everything. But I was so distracted, just sitting there at my desk fuming about my father-in-law leaving his golf clubs in the middle of the hallway again, that I completely missed a major exclusivity clause in the promoter's draft. We almost lost the entire deal. \n\nAnd because of that, my managing partner... we've known each other for fifteen years, we came up in the agency together, we've always had each other's backs... he actually pulled me into his office. He sat me down and looked me in the eye and questioned whether I could still be trusted with major negotiations right now. Me! I'm the closer. I'm the guy they bring in to seal the deal. To hear him say that, to see the doubt in his face... it was humiliating. But the worst part is, I couldn't even defend myself because I knew he was right. I'm totally dropping the ball. \n\nAnd it all comes back to the house. The conflict there has just gotten so intense, so completely unmanageable. My wife and I, we used to be such a solid team, but now every single interaction we have turns into a fight about her parents. It's gotten serious enough that she's actually the one who pushed for outside help. She basically told me I needed to go talk to someone—which is why I'm sitting here with you—because she can't handle how on edge I am, and I can't handle her parents taking over our lives, and... it's just a mess. My career, my marriage, it feels like it's all imploding just because I can't get a single moment of peace in my own home. So, yeah, it's definitely affecting my relationships across the board.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety and tension would significantly decrease if your in-laws were no longer living with you?\n\nPatient: Oh my god, yes. A thousand percent yes. If I could just get my house back, I know I would go back to normal almost instantly. I'd have my sanctuary back. But the thing you have to understand, the whole reason I'm in such a state right now, is that they are still there. \n\nThis isn't some past event that I'm just holding onto or stewing over. The living arrangement hasn't ended. It is an active, ongoing situation. I mean, if they had moved out months ago and I was still missing exclusivity clauses in major contracts and hiding in my bedroom, then yeah, maybe I'd think I had some deep-seated psychological issue we'd need to unpack. I'd be looking at myself going, what is wrong with you, man, the crisis is over! But the crisis isn't over. The threat is still inside the house, literally. \n\nEvery single morning I wake up and hear the coffee grinder going at 5:30 AM because my mother-in-law is an early riser, and my chest just tightens up all over again before I've even opened my eyes. Every evening, I'm dreading pulling into the driveway because I know they're inside. So, yes, my anxiety and the tension in my shoulders, all of it would absolutely plummet if they just packed up and left. \n\nBut since my wife refuses to set a hard move-out date, they are still living with us indefinitely. I have no timeline. In my business, you always have a timeline—a tour ends, a contract expires, a promotional cycle wraps up. There's always a light at the end of the tunnel. Here? Nothing. So all this stress, all these problems at work and the fights with my wife, they're happening right now because I am currently, actively living in this scenario every single day. It hasn't been resolved. If it were resolved, I wouldn't even be sitting in this chair right now, I can promise you that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your symptoms would resolve on their own if the stressful situation with your in-laws living with you were to end?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / agent_or_business_manager_of_artist_performer_or_athlete / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 2\nmanifestation: \"The living arrangement still hasn’t ended, so these problems are happening while the in-law situation is still active, not months after it was resolved.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Exactly, yeah. I mean, I know I basically just said this, but yes, one hundred percent. I really do believe everything would just... evaporate. Because the thing is, the living arrangement still hasn't ended. 

It’s not like I’m sitting here talking to you in November about some stressful event that wrapped up back in July, you know? If they had packed up and moved out to some condo in Boca months ago, and the house was finally quiet, and I was *still* snapping at my wife and forgetting exclusivity clauses in my artists' contracts... well, then I'd be the first to admit that something is fundamentally broken in me. I'd be sitting here saying, "Doc, the coast is clear but I'm still drowning, what's wrong with me?" 

But that's just not the reality here. These problems, the tension, the massive screw-ups at the agency, the constant feeling that my chest is going to cave in—they are all happening right now, specifically because the in-law situation is still completely active. They are physically in my house right now as we speak. Probably reorganizing my garage or complaining about how I set the thermostat. So I haven't even had the luxury of finding out if I'd bounce back on my own, because I'm still in the thick of it. I'm not dealing with the aftermath of a crisis months after it was resolved; I am actively reacting to a nightmare that is currently ongoing, day in and day out. If they actually left... yeah, I know I'd be fine. I just need them out.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know I keep hammering this exact same point home, and I apologize if I sound like a broken record, but... yes. Absolutely. 

Because, honestly? Occasional stress and anxiety? Doc, I'm an agent in the entertainment industry. My entire career is built on managing stress. When a tour bus breaks down in the middle of a blizzard, or a venue double-books a date, or an artist decides they suddenly hate their lead single three days before the album drops—that is stress. I eat that kind of stress for breakfast. I thrive on it. That kind of normal, everyday anxiety keeps me sharp, and I've been handling it perfectly fine for twenty years. It's manageable because it's just part of life, and it has boundaries. 

But this thing with my in-laws? This is entirely different. The reason my functioning is completely in the gutter right now—the reason my partner is questioning my competence and my wife is sending me to a psychiatrist—is precisely because this living arrangement still hasn't ended. It is an ongoing, twenty-four-seven invasion of my space. 

Like I was just saying, these problems I'm having, they are happening right now while the in-law situation is still completely active. I'm not sitting here struggling with the residual effects of something that got fixed a long time ago. If they had moved out, say, six months ago, and it was now months after the situation was completely resolved, and I was *still* dropping the ball on major contracts and fighting with my wife over every little thing... well, then I'd agree that my ability to function was just fundamentally broken. I'd admit I couldn't even handle regular life anymore. 

But that's not what's happening. The situation is still active. They are literally still in my guest room. They are still taking over my kitchen and my living room and my marriage. I haven't had a single day of relief to even try to get back to my baseline. So, if you take them out of the equation? If that specific, active crisis is actually resolved? Yes, I know for a fact I could handle the normal, everyday stress of my life and my job without a second thought. I've always been able to handle normal stress. I just need this specific, unending nightmare to actually end.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I... I guess I'm here because everything feels like it's completely unraveling, to be honest. It all really started when my in-laws moved into our house. We thought it would be manageable, you know, family first and we have the space, but ever since they brought their boxes in, my entire sense of peace is just gone. \n\nI’ve been so incredibly keyed up all the time, just this constant, buzzing tension from the minute I wake up, and at the exact same time, I'm just completely worn down. I have absolutely zero patience left. I’m snapping at my wife constantly over the smallest things, which isn't fair to her and just creates more tension in the house, but I just have no fuse left at all. \n\nBut what really scared me, what actually made me pick up the phone to make this appointment, is how it's destroying my work. I'm a talent manager, mostly for musicians, and my whole career is built on being the guy who handles things, the guy who is always on the ball. But lately? I’m looking at my phone ringing with calls I would normally handle immediately, and I’m just... avoiding them. I just sit there and watch it go to voicemail. It got so bad last week that I nearly let a major contract for one of my top musicians completely fall apart. I was supposed to finalize the terms, and I just sat there staring at the paperwork because I literally couldn’t think straight. My brain was just static. If I start losing my clients because I can't even function, I really don't know what I'm going to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I've had anything like that. I mean, I'm definitely tense, like I said, just this constant buzzing feeling, and my heart might beat a little faster when I hear my mother-in-law's footsteps coming down the hall—which, by the way, she has this very distinct, heavy walk that I can hear from anywhere in the house, it's maddening—but it's not like a sudden attack of terror or anything. It doesn't peak like that. It's more just... a continuous, exhausting layer of stress that never, ever lets up. \n\nI don't feel like I'm having a heart attack or like I'm suddenly dying, if that's what you mean. It's just that my baseline anxiety is so high right now that I can't think straight. But no sudden spikes out of nowhere, no. Just the constant, overwhelming pressure of them being in my space, rearranging my kitchen, making passive-aggressive comments about how I load the dishwasher... it's just always there, humming in the background, making it impossible to focus on my clients.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, activities, or situations because of your feelings of tension or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... not exactly in the way you might think. It's not like I'm terrified to go to the grocery store or drive my car or anything like that. But, if I'm being completely honest, and I guess that's why I'm paying to be here, I am definitely avoiding my own house as much as humanly possible. Which is ridiculous, right? It's my house. I pay the mortgage. But the second I pull into the driveway and see their car parked there—usually taking up way too much space, by the way, because my father-in-law cannot park straight to save his life—I just feel this knot in my stomach tighten up. \n\nSo, I've started finding excuses to stay at the office later and later. I'll tell my wife I have to review contracts, or that I'm waiting on a call from a promoter on the West Coast, which, sure, happens sometimes, but lately, I'm just sitting at my desk, staring at the wall, just to delay having to walk through my own front door. And even when I am home, I'm avoiding the common areas. I'll hide out in our bedroom, pretending I have to take a work call, just so I don't have to sit in the living room and listen to my mother-in-law critique whatever we're watching on TV. \n\nAnd work... I mentioned work before, right? I'm definitely avoiding things there, too. Not the physical office, but the actual work. Like I said, I'm avoiding phone calls. I'm avoiding emails from clients who I know are going to be demanding, because I just don't have the mental bandwidth to deal with them right now. Usually, I love the back-and-forth, the negotiation, the putting out fires—that's what makes me good at my job. But right now? If a client emails me with a problem, I just close the laptop. I can't even look at it. I just want to put my head down and make it all stop. So, yeah, I guess I'm avoiding a lot, just... not necessarily places, if that makes sense. Just the situations that require me to actually engage and deal with people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of sadness or hopelessness lately?\n\nPatient: Sadness? Hopelessness? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, it's not like I'm sitting around crying or feeling like life isn't worth living or anything dramatic like that. It's really not sadness at all. If anything, it's just... frustration. Pure, unadulterated frustration. \n\nI don't feel hopeless, exactly, because I know logically that they can't live with us forever, right? I mean, surely there's an end date to this, even if my wife won't commit to one when I try to bring it up. She just says, \"They're family, we have to help them right now,\" which, fine, I get it, but what about helping me? What about my sanity? So it's not hopeless, it's just... I'm just so angry and on edge all the time. \n\nI'm not depressed, I don't think. I still want to do things, I still want to be successful at my job—I mean, I'm terrified of losing my clients, which wouldn't happen if I didn't care, right? If I were hopeless, I'd just let the contracts expire and not give a damn. But I do care. I care a lot. It's just that I'm so bogged down by this constant buzzing anxiety and irritation that I can't execute. So, no, definitely not sad. Just incredibly, overwhelmingly stressed out and resentful that my sanctuary has been completely hijacked by people who think it's perfectly acceptable to rearrange my spice rack alphabetically when I specifically had it organized by cuisine. Who does that? It's infuriating, not sad.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty maintaining your relationships with others outside of your family due to your current stress and anxiety?\n\nPatient: Outside of my family? Yeah. I mean, absolutely. It's bleeding into literally everything. You'd think I could compartmentalize, right? That's what I do for a living, I manage artists, I handle crises, I keep the drama out of the business. But right now... it's like the dam broke. \n\nJust last week—and this is what really terrified me, honestly—I nearly lost a massive contract for a musician I represent. He's this incredibly talented guy, but very high maintenance, always tweaking his tour rider, and usually, I am all over those details. I catch everything. But I was so distracted, just sitting there at my desk fuming about my father-in-law leaving his golf clubs in the middle of the hallway again, that I completely missed a major exclusivity clause in the promoter's draft. We almost lost the entire deal. \n\nAnd because of that, my managing partner... we've known each other for fifteen years, we came up in the agency together, we've always had each other's backs... he actually pulled me into his office. He sat me down and looked me in the eye and questioned whether I could still be trusted with major negotiations right now. Me! I'm the closer. I'm the guy they bring in to seal the deal. To hear him say that, to see the doubt in his face... it was humiliating. But the worst part is, I couldn't even defend myself because I knew he was right. I'm totally dropping the ball. \n\nAnd it all comes back to the house. The conflict there has just gotten so intense, so completely unmanageable. My wife and I, we used to be such a solid team, but now every single interaction we have turns into a fight about her parents. It's gotten serious enough that she's actually the one who pushed for outside help. She basically told me I needed to go talk to someone—which is why I'm sitting here with you—because she can't handle how on edge I am, and I can't handle her parents taking over our lives, and... it's just a mess. My career, my marriage, it feels like it's all imploding just because I can't get a single moment of peace in my own home. So, yeah, it's definitely affecting my relationships across the board.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety and tension would significantly decrease if your in-laws were no longer living with you?\n\nPatient: Oh my god, yes. A thousand percent yes. If I could just get my house back, I know I would go back to normal almost instantly. I'd have my sanctuary back. But the thing you have to understand, the whole reason I'm in such a state right now, is that they are still there. \n\nThis isn't some past event that I'm just holding onto or stewing over. The living arrangement hasn't ended. It is an active, ongoing situation. I mean, if they had moved out months ago and I was still missing exclusivity clauses in major contracts and hiding in my bedroom, then yeah, maybe I'd think I had some deep-seated psychological issue we'd need to unpack. I'd be looking at myself going, what is wrong with you, man, the crisis is over! But the crisis isn't over. The threat is still inside the house, literally. \n\nEvery single morning I wake up and hear the coffee grinder going at 5:30 AM because my mother-in-law is an early riser, and my chest just tightens up all over again before I've even opened my eyes. Every evening, I'm dreading pulling into the driveway because I know they're inside. So, yes, my anxiety and the tension in my shoulders, all of it would absolutely plummet if they just packed up and left. \n\nBut since my wife refuses to set a hard move-out date, they are still living with us indefinitely. I have no timeline. In my business, you always have a timeline—a tour ends, a contract expires, a promotional cycle wraps up. There's always a light at the end of the tunnel. Here? Nothing. So all this stress, all these problems at work and the fights with my wife, they're happening right now because I am currently, actively living in this scenario every single day. It hasn't been resolved. If it were resolved, I wouldn't even be sitting in this chair right now, I can promise you that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms would resolve on their own if the stressful situation with your in-laws living with you were to end?\n\nPatient: Exactly, yeah. I mean, I know I basically just said this, but yes, one hundred percent. I really do believe everything would just... evaporate. Because the thing is, the living arrangement still hasn't ended. \n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting here talking to you in November about some stressful event that wrapped up back in July, you know? If they had packed up and moved out to some condo in Boca months ago, and the house was finally quiet, and I was *still* snapping at my wife and forgetting exclusivity clauses in my artists' contracts... well, then I'd be the first to admit that something is fundamentally broken in me. I'd be sitting here saying, \"Doc, the coast is clear but I'm still drowning, what's wrong with me?\" \n\nBut that's just not the reality here. These problems, the tension, the massive screw-ups at the agency, the constant feeling that my chest is going to cave in—they are all happening right now, specifically because the in-law situation is still completely active. They are physically in my house right now as we speak. Probably reorganizing my garage or complaining about how I set the thermostat. So I haven't even had the luxury of finding out if I'd bounce back on my own, because I'm still in the thick of it. I'm not dealing with the aftermath of a crisis months after it was resolved; I am actively reacting to a nightmare that is currently ongoing, day in and day out. If they actually left... yeah, I know I'd be fine. I just need them out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to return to your normal level of functioning at work and in your relationships if the situation with your in-laws was resolved, but you still experienced some occasional stress or anxiety in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I know I keep hammering this exact same point home, and I apologize if I sound like a broken record, but... yes. Absolutely. \n\nBecause, honestly? Occasional stress and anxiety? Doc, I'm an agent in the entertainment industry. My entire career is built on managing stress. When a tour bus breaks down in the middle of a blizzard, or a venue double-books a date, or an artist decides they suddenly hate their lead single three days before the album drops—that is stress. I eat that kind of stress for breakfast. I thrive on it. That kind of normal, everyday anxiety keeps me sharp, and I've been handling it perfectly fine for twenty years. It's manageable because it's just part of life, and it has boundaries. \n\nBut this thing with my in-laws? This is entirely different. The reason my functioning is completely in the gutter right now—the reason my partner is questioning my competence and my wife is sending me to a psychiatrist—is precisely because this living arrangement still hasn't ended. It is an ongoing, twenty-four-seven invasion of my space. \n\nLike I was just saying, these problems I'm having, they are happening right now while the in-law situation is still completely active. I'm not sitting here struggling with the residual effects of something that got fixed a long time ago. If they had moved out, say, six months ago, and it was now months after the situation was completely resolved, and I was *still* dropping the ball on major contracts and fighting with my wife over every little thing... well, then I'd agree that my ability to function was just fundamentally broken. I'd admit I couldn't even handle regular life anymore. \n\nBut that's not what's happening. The situation is still active. They are literally still in my guest room. They are still taking over my kitchen and my living room and my marriage. I haven't had a single day of relief to even try to get back to my baseline. So, if you take them out of the equation? If that specific, active crisis is actually resolved? Yes, I know for a fact I could handle the normal, everyday stress of my life and my job without a second thought. I've always been able to handle normal stress. I just need this specific, unending nightmare to actually end.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it’s... God, if you ask my brain, it feels like it’s been a decade, but looking at the actual calendar? It’s been about a month. Just one month. Can you believe that? This whole nightmare, all of this overwhelming stress, it has been going on for about a month, exactly since my in-laws moved into our home. 

They arrived right at the start of the month, pulling up with a rental van full of boxes and this... this attitude like they were moving into a resort where I'm the concierge. And literally from day one, from the very first night they slept down the hall in the guest room, this heavy, suffocating tension just clamped down on me and hasn't let go. 

In my line of work, a month is nothing, right? A month is a blink of an eye. When you're planning a fifty-city arena tour, a month is just the time it takes to get the pyrotechnics approved by the fire marshal. It flies by. But this past month? Living in my own house with them? It has dragged on so painfully slowly. Every single day of these last four weeks has felt like an absolute marathon. I wake up, I'm stressed. I go to the office, I'm stressed because I'm exhausted from the morning. I come home, I'm stressed because they're sitting in my living room watching game shows at maximum volume and judging what time I walked through the door. 

And honestly, the fact that it’s only been four weeks is what terrifies me the most right now. If my life and my work are falling apart this badly after just one single month of them being in the house, what happens at month two? Month three? Because, like I keep saying, my wife won't give them a deadline to leave. They just showed up a month ago, unpacked their endless bags, and my entire reality just derailed.

I mentioned work before, right? I'm definitely avoiding things there, too. Not the physical office, but the actual work. Like I said, I'm avoiding phone calls. I'm avoiding emails from clients who I know are going to be demanding, because I just don't have the mental bandwidth to deal with them right now. Usually, I love the back-and-forth, the negotiation, the putting out fires—that's what makes me good at my job. But right now? If a client emails me with a problem, I just close the laptop. I can't even look at it. I just want to put my head down and make it all stop. So, yeah, I guess I'm avoiding a lot, just... not necessarily places, if that makes sense. Just the situations that require me to actually engage and deal with people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of sadness or hopelessness lately?\n\nPatient: Sadness? Hopelessness? No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, it's not like I'm sitting around crying or feeling like life isn't worth living or anything dramatic like that. It's really not sadness at all. Yeah. I mean, absolutely. It's bleeding into literally everything. You'd think I could compartmentalize, right? I'm totally dropping the ball. \n\nAnd it all comes back to the house. The conflict there has just gotten so intense, so completely unmanageable. My wife and I, we used to be such a solid team, but now every single interaction we have turns into a fight about her parents. It's gotten serious enough that she's actually the one who pushed for outside help. She basically told me I needed to go talk to someone—which is why I'm sitting here with you—because she can't handle how on edge I am, and I can't handle her parents taking over our lives, and... it's just a mess. My career, my marriage, it feels like it's all imploding just because I can't get a single moment of peace in my own home. So, yeah, it's definitely affecting my relationships across the board.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety and tension would significantly decrease if your in-laws were no longer living with you?\n\nPatient: Oh my god, yes. A thousand percent yes. If I could just get my house back, I know I would go back to normal almost instantly. I'd have my sanctuary back. But the thing you have to understand, the whole reason I'm in such a state right now, is that they are still there. \n\nThis isn't some past event that I'm just holding onto or stewing over. The living arrangement hasn't ended. It is an active, ongoing situation. I mean, if they had moved out months ago and I was still missing exclusivity clauses in major contracts and hiding in my bedroom, then yeah, maybe I'd think I had some deep-seated psychological issue we'd need to unpack. I'd be looking at myself going, what is wrong with you, man, the crisis is over! But the crisis isn't over. Every evening, I'm dreading pulling into the driveway because I know they're inside. So, yes, my anxiety and the tension in my shoulders, all of it would absolutely plummet if they just packed up and left. \n\nBut since my wife refuses to set a hard move-out date, they are still living with us indefinitely. I have no timeline. In my business, you always have a timeline—a tour ends, a contract expires, a promotional cycle wraps up. There's always a light at the end of the tunnel. Here? Nothing. So all this stress, all these problems at work and the fights with my wife, they're happening right now because I am currently, actively living in this scenario every single day. It hasn't been resolved. If it were resolved, I wouldn't even be sitting in this chair right now, I can promise you that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms would resolve on their own if the stressful situation with your in-laws living with you were to end?\n\nPatient: Exactly, yeah. I mean, I know I basically just said this, but yes, one hundred percent. I really do believe everything would just... evaporate. Because the thing is, the living arrangement still hasn't ended. \n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting here talking to you in November about some stressful event that wrapped up back in July, you know? If they had packed up and moved out to some condo in Boca months ago, and the house was finally quiet, and I was *still* snapping at my wife and forgetting exclusivity clauses in my artists' contracts... well, then I'd be the first to admit that something is fundamentally broken in me. I'd be sitting here saying, \"Doc, the coast is clear but I'm still drowning, what's wrong with me?\" \n\nBut that's just not the reality here. These problems, the tension, the massive screw-ups at the agency, the constant feeling that my chest is going to cave in—they are all happening right now, specifically because the in-law situation is still completely active. They are physically in my house right now as we speak. Probably reorganizing my garage or complaining about how I set the thermostat. So I haven't even had the luxury of finding out if I'd bounce back on my own, because I'm still in the thick of it. I'm not dealing with the aftermath of a crisis months after it was resolved; I am actively reacting to a nightmare that is currently ongoing, day in and day out. If they actually left... yeah, I know I'd be fine. I just need them out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to return to your normal level of functioning at work and in your relationships if the situation with your in-laws was resolved, but you still experienced some occasional stress or anxiety in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I know I keep hammering this exact same point home, and I apologize if I sound like a broken record, but... yes. Absolutely. \n\nBecause, honestly? Occasional stress and anxiety? Doc, I'm an agent in the entertainment industry. My entire career is built on managing stress. When a tour bus breaks down in the middle of a blizzard, or a venue double-books a date, or an artist decides they suddenly hate their lead single three days before the album drops—that is stress. I eat that kind of stress for breakfast. I thrive on it. That kind of normal, everyday anxiety keeps me sharp, and I've been handling it perfectly fine for twenty years. It's manageable because it's just part of life, and it has boundaries. \n\nBut this thing with my in-laws? This is entirely different. The reason my functioning is completely in the gutter right now—the reason my partner is questioning my competence and my wife is sending me to a psychiatrist—is precisely because this living arrangement still hasn't ended. It is an ongoing, twenty-four-seven invasion of my space. \n\nLike I was just saying, these problems I'm having, they are happening right now while the in-law situation is still completely active. I'm not sitting here struggling with the residual effects of something that got fixed a long time ago. I'd admit I couldn't even handle regular life anymore. \n\nBut that's not what's happening. The situation is still active. They are literally still in my guest room. They are still taking over my kitchen and my living room and my marriage. I haven't had a single day of relief to even try to get back to my baseline. So, if you take them out of the equation? If that specific, active crisis is actually resolved? Yes, I know for a fact I could handle the normal, everyday stress of my life and my job without a second thought. I've always been able to handle normal stress. I just need this specific, unending nightmare to actually end.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this overwhelming stress and anxiety since your in-laws moved in?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s... God, if you ask my brain, it feels like it’s been a decade, but looking at the actual calendar? It’s been about a month. Just one month. Can you believe that? This whole nightmare, all of this overwhelming stress, it has been going on for about a month, exactly since my in-laws moved into our home. \n\nThey arrived right at the start of the month, pulling up with a rental van full of boxes and this... this attitude like they were moving into a resort where I'm the concierge. And literally from day one, from the very first night they slept down the hall in the guest room, this heavy, suffocating tension just clamped down on me and hasn't let go. \n\nIn my line of work, a month is nothing, right? A month is a blink of an eye. When you're planning a fifty-city arena tour, a month is just the time it takes to get the pyrotechnics approved by the fire marshal. It flies by. But this past month? Living in my own house with them? It has dragged on so painfully slowly. Every single day of these last four weeks has felt like an absolute marathon. I wake up, I'm stressed. A month is a blink of an eye. When you're planning a fifty-city arena tour, a month is just the time it takes to get the pyrotechnics approved by the fire marshal. It flies by. But this past month? Living in my own house with them? It has dragged on so painfully slowly. Every single day of these last four weeks has felt like an absolute marathon. I wake up, I'm stressed. I go to the office, I'm stressed because I'm exhausted from the morning. I come home, I'm stressed because they're sitting in my living room watching game shows at maximum volume and judging what time I walked through the door. \n\nAnd honestly, the fact that it’s only been four weeks is what terrifies me the most right now. If my life and my work are falling apart this badly after just one single month of them being in the house, what happens at month two? Month three? Because, like I keep saying, my wife won't give them a deadline to leave. They just showed up a month ago, unpacked their endless bags, and my entire reality just derailed.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your symptoms been present for less than 6 months?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / agent_or_business_manager_of_artist_performer_or_athlete / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 2\nmanifestation: \"The living arrangement still hasn’t ended, so these problems are happening while the in-law situation is still active, not months after it was resolved.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Six months? Oh, man. Doc, if this had been going on for six months, I wouldn't even be sitting here in this chair right now. I’d be divorced, or I’d have walked out into traffic, or my agency would have fired me for gross negligence. So, yes, definitely less than six months. Like I was just saying a minute ago, it’s only been about four weeks since they showed up and completely hijacked my life. 

But I think the most important thing to understand here—and I know we keep circling around this, but I really need to make sure I'm being perfectly clear about the reality of my day-to-day—is that the living arrangement still hasn’t ended. We are right in the middle of it. So these problems, the absolute trainwreck my brain has become, they are happening while the in-law situation is still completely, one-hundred-percent active. 

I mean, it would be a totally different conversation if they had moved out back in the spring, right? If they had packed up and left, and I was sitting here in your office still unable to function, still messing up my clients' contracts, still snapping at everyone... well, then we'd be talking about something else entirely. But I'm not dealing with this months after it was resolved. There is no resolution yet. I'm not stuck in the past; I'm stuck in the present. The stress is ongoing because they are literally still sleeping down the hall. I'm waking up to it every single day. So, yeah, way less than six months, but only because we are just at month one of a hostage situation that has no end date in sight.
